RESEARCH HIGHLIGHTS
 In our DOE-funded project, we made significant progress employing surface reporter molecules and
in situ surface spectroscopy to investigate electrochemical and photoelectrochemical interfaces, as
described below. In this section, we present some of our main findings. In addition to Raman spectroscopy,
we also used Second Harmonic Generation (SHG) spectroscopy to probe interfacial electric fields, surface
potential, and charge transfer at electrode and photoelectrode interfaces. SHG complements Raman by
eliminating the need for plasmonic nanoparticles, enabling studies of homogeneous surfaces.
 1.) Measuring Local Fields and Photo-induced Surface Potentials using Surface Reporter Molecule
 4-MBN13-16
 In our DOE-funded work, we utilized a spectroscopic technique for monitoring the photovoltages
 produced at semiconductor/liquid junctions.13-16 Surface reporter molecule mercaptobenzonitrile (MBN)
 was functionalized on the photoelectrode surface (p-type silicon) and was measured using in situ surface
 enhanced Raman scattering (SERS) spectroscopy with a water immersion lens under electrochemical
 working conditions. In particular, the vibrational frequency of the C≡N stretch mode (𝜔CN ) around 2225
 cm-1 is sensitive to the local electric field in solution at the electrode/electrolyte interface via the vibrational
 Stark effect.{Shi, 2018 #3} Over the applied potential range (-0.8 V to 0.6 V vs. Ag/AgCl), we observe
 (at low laser power), with a Stark tuning rate of d/dV = 5.668),

 2.) Measuring Local pH and pKa using Surface Enhanced Raman Spectroscopy of 4-MBA6,1
 Establishing the local pH/ion concentration near an electrode surface has been a longstanding problem
 in electrochemistry and photoelectrochemistry. We recently reported spectroscopic measurements of the
 local pH and pKa at the surface of an electrode/electrolyte interface using surface enhanced Raman
 scattering (SERS) spectroscopy of 4-mercaptobenzoic acid (4-MBA), as illustrated in Figure 3a.6,1 In acidic
 and basic environments, the protonated and deprotonated carboxyl functional groups exist in the solution
 as -COOH and -COO-, which have different Raman active vibrational features at around 1697 cm -1 and
 1414 cm-1, respectively, as indicated in Figure 3b. In pH-neutral water, as the applied electrochemical
 potential is varied from negative to positive, the acidic form of the 4-MBA (i.e., COOH) decreases in Raman
 intensity, and the basic form (i.e., COO-) increases in
 COO − [H + per volume ]
 Raman intensity, as plotted in Figure 3c. The change pK a = − log [COOH ]
 (1)
 in local pH is due to the application of electrochemical
 −
 potentials and the accumulation of ions near the pK a,surface = − log COO (%) + pH (2)
 [COOH (%)]
 electrode. Under various applied potentials, the ratio
 of the 1697 cm-1 and 1587 cm-1 (pH-independent) peaks spans the range between 0.7 and 0, and the ratio of
 the 1414 cm-1 and 1587 cm-1 peaks ranges from 0 to 30. By fitting this data to a normalized sigmoid function,
 we can obtain the percentage of surface protonation/deprotonation (plotted in Figure 3d), which can be
 related to the pKa and pH of the system, as described in Eqs. 1 and 2. Thus, we can measure the local pH at
 the electrode surface using the relative Raman intensities of the 4-MBA. Since pH is an inherently three-
 dimensional quantity, there is no straightforward method for quantifying this at an inherently two-
 dimensional interface. Here, we have developed a framework for determining pKa, which is a well-defined
 quantity at the 2D electrode surface by using surface-bound molecules that report pKa within 1 nm of the
 surface. In our future work, we will extend this study to semiconductor surfaces under illumination.
 Additionally, we will explore the impact of various surfactant molecules (e.g., CTAB) and buffer systems
  (a) (e.g., KHCOOO3) with and without dissolving

5.) Probing Semiconductor/Electrolyte Interfaces with Surface Reporter Molecules: Evidence for
Zero-Field Regions and Non-equilibrium Electrochemical Behavior
 We have investigated the nature of electric field in the accumulation regime. Based on our ability to
probe band bending with 4-mercaptobenzonitrile (4-MBN), we observed a persistent zero-field region over
a wide range of applied potentials. We found that this behavior arises from the shift in equilibration between
majority and minority carriers with the electrolyte. It has contrasted with the behavior of metal electrodes
such as fluorine-doped tin oxide (FTO) and indium tin oxide (ITO), which have exhibited a linearly
increasing electric field as the electrochemical potential has risen, reflecting a transition from anion-
dominated conditions at low (reducing) potentials to cation-dominated conditions at high (oxidizing)
potentials. For semiconductor electrodes, two distinct behaviors have been observed: (1) monotonic Stark
shifts with electrochemical potential in leaky junctions, and (2) vibrational frequencies that have saturated
near ∼2225 cm-1, indicative of a zero electric field, in more diode-like junctions. These findings have
suggested a complex equilibrium—or nonequilibrium—balance between the semiconductor’s band
structure and the ionic composition of the surrounding solution, with important implications for charge
transfer and field-driven processes in electrochemical and photoelectrochemical systems.

Figure 6: Raman shift of the C≡N vibrational mode plotted as a function of applied potential using different samples,
including (a) 5 nm Al2O3 on ITO/FTO, (b) 5 nm TiO2 on p-type InP and 5 nm Al2O3 on n-type silicon, and (c) 5 nm Al₂O₃
on p-type silicon, 5 nm TiO2 on n-type InP, and 5 nm Al₂O₃ on FTO. The flat-band potentials (FBP) are indicated,
illustrating the correlation between interfacial electric field and vibrational Stark shifts across different semiconductor–
oxide systems.

 6

6.) Monitoring photovoltages produced at p-InP semiconductor/liquid interfaces using in situ
surface-enhanced Raman scattering (SERS) spectroscopy
 Since p-InP has less trapped states and is produces more photovoltage than other bulk semiconductors
such as Si, GaAs, we used stark shift photovoltage measurement technique to measure the photovoltage in
p-InP photoelectrodes. Increasing the laser power from 7.7 mW to 800 mW produced a photoinduced shift
in the C≡N stretch Raman mode, ΔνCN, of up to 5 cm⁻¹, corresponding to a photovoltage of 0.4 V generated
at the semiconductor/liquid junction. A comparison was made with conventional photovoltage
measurements obtained by measuring the shift in the onset potential of the semiconductor photoelectrodes
under illuminated and dark conditions. While the conventional photovoltage measurement approach was
only valid at very reducing potentials with the semiconductor in deep depletion, this SERS-based approach
enabled measurement of the photovoltage generated within the semiconductor over the full range of
electrochemical potentials. Fundamentally, these measurements revealed how minority and majority
carriers in semiconductors reach equilibrium with the redox potential of the electrolyte under illumination.

Figure 7: (a) Raman shifts of the CN stretch mode taken on p-type indium phosphide under various applied potentials
plotted as a function of laser power. (b) Maximum photoinduced change in Raman shift (ΔωCN) and corresponding
photovoltage plotted as a function of the applied potential. (c) I–V curves of the p-InP/TiO2/Au photocathode in 1 M
HCl, where ΔV = 0.4 V (illumination conditions: 67 mW laser).

7.) Photovoltage Mapping at Semiconductor/Electrolyte Interfaces via Surface Reporter Molecules:
From Depletion to Accumulation
 Semiconductors buried in electrolyte can be in depletion, accumulation, or inversion region depending
on the applied potential. We used our spectroscopic approach to monitor photovoltages at
semiconductor/liquid junctions of n-type and p-type silicon photoelectrodes. The laser power dependences
of the Raman shift of the C≡N stretch mode on n-type and p-type silicon showed opposite trends.
Specifically, at negative electrochemical potentials, n-Si exhibited a photoinduced blueshift of ωCN, whereas
p-Si exhibited a redshift. Conversely, at positive electrochemical potentials, n-Si exhibited a photoinduced
redshift of ωCN, whereas p-Si exhibited a blueshift. This inverse behavior reflected the opposite band
bending in n- and p-type silicon at the electrochemical interface and the opposite photovoltages produced
at the semiconductor/liquid junction. The transition from positive to negative photovoltages above and
below the flat-band potential (for n-type silicon) reflected a change in the polarity of ions in solution
comprising the Helmholtz double layer. While the behavior of the semiconductor/electrolyte interface under
depletion conditions was relatively straightforward to interpret, its behavior under accumulation conditions
was more surprising and reflected a shift from minority carriers being in equilibrium with solution-phase
ions to majority carriers reaching equilibrium with oppositely charged ions in solution.

 7

Figure 8: (a) In depletion mode (V > -0.07 V vs. Ag/AgCl), photoexcited minority holes are swept out to the solution
where they equilibrate with the OER redox potential (i.e., 1.23 V vs. NHE). The illustrated band bending corresponds
to an applied potential of +0.6 V vs Ag/AgCl. (b) At the flat-band potential (V = -0.07 V vs. Ag/AgCl), there is no band
bending within the semiconductor, however, some trapped charge can exist in the oxide layer at this potential. (c) In
accumulation mode (V < -0.07 V vs. Ag/AgCl), photoexcited majority electrons are swept to the solution where they
equilibrate with the HER redox potential (i.e., ΔV vs. NHE).

8.) Direct In Situ Measurement of Quantum Efficiencies of Charge Separation and Proton Reduction
at TiO2‐Protected GaP Photocathodes
 We developed a method of identifying the microscopic efficiency loss processes in semiconductor
photoelectrodes. Our in situ transient photocurrent and transient reflectance spectroscopy (TRS)
measurements of titanium dioxide–protected gallium phosphide photocathodes enabled the direct probing
of separated charge carriers responsible for water reduction and allowed their kinetics to be followed.
Comparison with transient photocurrent measurements enabled direct probing of the initial charge
separation quantum efficiency (ϕCS) and provided support for a transient photocurrent model that divided
IPCE into the product of quantum efficiencies of light absorption (ϕabs), charge separation (ϕCN), and
photoreduction (ϕred), i.e., IPCE = ϕabsϕCNϕred. Our study showed that two general key loss pathways existed:
recombination within the bulk GaP, which reduced ϕCS, and interfacial recombination at the junction, which
decreased ϕred. Although both loss pathways could be reduced at more negative applied bias, for GaP/TiO₂
the initial charge separation loss was identified as the key efficiency-limiting factor. Our combined transient
reflectance and photocurrent study provided a time-resolved view of the microscopic steps involved in the
overall light-to-current conversion process and delivered detailed insights into the main loss pathways of
the photoelectrochemical system.

Figure 9: Transient reflectance spectra and kinetics of GaP/TiO2 measured as a function of average pulsed excitation
power density. (a) Excitation power dependence of TR spectra and kinetics of GaP/TiO 2 in the FKO signal region
averaged at 100-200 ps at -1.5 V. (b) Transient FKO amplitude as a function of average excitation power densities
measured at 0, -0.8, and -1.5 V, showing saturation of signal amplitude at higher fluences. The FKO amplitudes in (d)
are monitored by subtracting 2.78 and 2.68 eV at a delay time of 50 ps, with ∼0.03 eV averaging spectral window
around each point to reduce the noise.

 8

9.) Nanoscale TiO2 Protection Layer Enhanced the Built‐In Field and Charge Separation
Performance of GaP Photoelectrodes
 We directly probed the interfacial field enhancement of p-GaP electrodes protected by n-TiO2 and its
effect on charge carriers by transient reflectance spectroscopy. We reported that increasing the TiO2 layer
thickness from 0 to 35 nm increases the field in the GaP depletion region, enhancing the rate and efficiency
of interfacial electron transfer from the GaP to TiO2 on the ps time scale as well as retarding interfacial
recombination on the microsecond time scale. This study demonstrated a general method for providing a
microscopic view of the photogenerated charge carrier’s pathway and loss mechanisms from the bulk of
the electrode to the long-lived separated charge at the interface that ultimately drives the
photoelectrochemical reactions.

Figure 10: Simulation of photogenerated charge carrier kinetics. (a) Schematic diagram of the numerical simulation
of a GaP/TiO2 p-n junction. (b) Comparison of simulated kinetics of the electron transfer from the GaP to the TiO 2
(black line) and measured FKO kinetics (color circles) of bare GaP, GaP/ TiO 2 (5 nm), and GaP/ TiO2 (35 nm). The
depletion layer (DL) width used for simulation is shown in the legend.

10.) Performance Enhancement of TiO2‐encapsulated Photoelectrodes Based on III–V Compound
Semiconductors
 Our book chapter reviewed strategies for enhancing the performance and stability of III–V
semiconductor photoelectrodes through ultrathin TiO2 encapsulation layers deposited by atomic layer
deposition. It shows that TiO2 passivation suppresses photocorrosion while enabling efficient charge
transport via tunneling and built-in electric fields at p–n heterojunctions formed between TiO2 and III–V
semiconductors such as InP, GaP, and GaAs. The chapter demonstrates that Ti3+ defect states (oxygen
vacancies) in amorphous TiO2 play a dual role by promoting interfacial charge transfer and lowering
reaction barriers for hydrogen evolution and CO2 reduction. Photoelectrochemical measurements,
spectroscopy, and band-diagram analysis reveal that performance enhancements arise from reduced surface
recombination, increased photovoltage, and improved selectivity when combined with cocatalysts or
plasmonic nanostructures. Overall, the work establishes TiO2 encapsulation as a general and tunable
approach for stabilizing high-performance semiconductor photoelectrodes while providing mechanistic
insight into charge separation, recombination, and interfacial reaction pathways in photoelectrochemical
systems.

Figure 11: (a) J–V measurements and (b) Faradaic efficiencies for methanol production with bare InP nanopillars and
InP/Cu and InP/TiO2/Cu photocathodes under 532 nm monochromatic irradiance in CO2-saturated 0.5 M KCl solution.
The dashed line indicates the potential applied during the methanol production experiment.
